of new money are needed to keep a bubble from popping. Eventually all
the debt associated with the new money becomes too great a burden for
the economy and everything collapses.\
</span>

##### ii. There is a diminishing return to taking on debt.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the United States, we have operated
on Keynesian principles since World War II. The government has printed
money. Debt levels have grown. We have not only gotten inflations and
bubbles. We have also gotten less and less growth for each increment of
debt.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">During the decade 1950–1959, we added
\$338 billion in debt, and we got 73¢ in economic growth (increase in
gross domestic product) for each \$1 in new debt. For the decade
1990–1999, we added \$12.5 trillion in debt, but got only 31¢ of growth
per dollar of debt. For the seven plus years 2000–2008 (1st quarter), we
added \$24.3 trillion in debt, but got only 19¢ in growth for every
dollar of debt.</span> <a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-23"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-23-back"><sup>23</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It thus required more and more debt to
generate further growth.\
</span>

##### iii. Eventually the return on debt becomes negative.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">By the end of 2007, the debt machine
shuddered and threatened to fail. Fed Chairman Bernanke immediately
reduced interest rates to emergency levels. Wall Street took one last
gulp of cheap credit. But it did not work. The crash came anyway.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">What is actually happening here? Henry
Hazlitt again explains:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">If one truth concerning economic crises
has been established . . . it is that they are typically brought on by
cheap money—i.e., low interest rate policies that encourage excessive
borrowing, excessive credit expansion, imprudent speculation, and all
the distortions and instabilities in the economy that these finally
bring about. . . . A policy of perpetual cheap money \[produces\] boom
and bust, \[not the perpetual quasi-boom that Keynes promised\].</span>
<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-24"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-24-back"><sup>24</sup></a>

#### 9a. Keynes: High interest rates caused the Depression.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">I attribute the slump of 1930, \[the
Great Depression\] primarily to the . . . effects . . . of dear
\[expensive\] money which preceded the stock market collapse \[of
1929\], and only secondarily to the collapse itself.</span>
<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-25"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-25-back"><sup>25</sup></a>

#### 9b. Comment: On the contrary, cheap loans produced, first a bubble, and then a Crash in 1929.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In Keynes’s version of events, it was
fear of the boom that led the US Federal Reserve to raise already too
high interest rates in the late 1920s. This supposedly choked off the
boom and led to the Great Depression.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The best short account of what really
led to the Great Depression is found in Paul Johnson’s</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">Modern
Times</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">. Johnson in turn drew
heavily on economist Murray Rothbard’s</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">America’s Great
Depression</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, the most
authoritative source. Rothbard drew to some degree on Lionel Robbins’s
book of the same title.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Both Rothbard and Robbins were in turn
influenced by Austrian economists Ludwig von Mises and his protégé
Friedrich</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">Hayek, although Robbins
later converted to Keynesianism. An excellent recent account of the
Depression is Amity Shlaes’s</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">The Forgotten
Man</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">What these books reveal is that the US
Federal Reserve did indeed bring us the Great Depression, but not in the
way that Keynes said. The Crash of 1929 was caused by the US Federal
Reserve’s cheap loan policy throughout the 1920s. Low interest rates
blew up a bubble remarkably similar to the US stock market and housing
bubbles that began in the 1990s. The Crash of 1929 presaged the Crash of
2008 seventy-nine years later.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">There are indeed many parallels between
the two crashes. In both cases, the primary cause was reckless central
bank policies that blew up unsustainable bubbles. In both cases, central
banks were following what came to be known as Keynesian policies. We
will discuss the Depression further in subsequent
chapters.</span><span class="calibre12 calibre3 calibre7 sgc-1"><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-22"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_nte-22-back"><sup>EE<br />
</sup></a></span>

#### 10a. Keynes: Booms are not wasteful.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The idea that investment funds will be
wasted on unsound and speculative ventures during a boom is a</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">serious
error.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-26"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-26-back"><sup>26</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Businesses may get a bit carried away
and invest in ventures which will not earn enough to cover</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">excessive\[ly\] high \[interest
costs\].</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-27"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-27-back"><sup>27</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But the solution to that problem is to
lower interest costs. In general, so long as humanity remains poor,
almost any investment is better than no investment at all, which is what
a slump will bring.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-28"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-28-back"><sup>28</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The conclusion is simple:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We should avoid \[high interest rates\]
. . . as we would hell-fire.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-29"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-29-back"><sup>29</sup></a>

#### 10b. Comment: Contrary to Keynes, bubbles are extremely wasteful.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A large quantity of investment, all else
being equal, is a plus. Investment raises our standard of living and
helps eliminate poverty. But it is quality of investment that counts
most.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Business investments that are
unproductive, or that fail to pay for themselves, are wasteful.
Unfortunately, these are just the kind of investments that we get during
a bubble. We get “dotcom” businesses with no earnings and no prospects
for future earnings. Or we get masses of housing that will not improve
productivity and that, even worse, will turn out to be unneeded or
unaffordable.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A rational and useful investment meets
the test of paying for itself under a regime of normal, market interest
rates. That is what the market test is for—to help weed out irrational
and wasteful ideas.\
</span>

#### 11a. Keynes: We need not worry that creating and sustaining booms with low interest rates will lead to inflation.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is unlikely that a boom will lead to
true inflation.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-30"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-30-back"><sup>30</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Booms may create</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">bottle-necks</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">in which the price of some products
rise.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-31"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-31-back"><sup>31</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But we cannot</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">declare that conditions of inflation
have set in</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">until unemployment has completely
disappeared.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-32"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-32-back"><sup>32</sup></a>

#### 11b. Comment: The idea that inflation appears all at once when the last unemployed person gets a job is preposterous. The record of the 1970s refutes it.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Characteristically, Keynes did not
define full employment. Sir William Beveridge, a Keynesian whose
“Beveridge Report” laid the foundation for the British welfare state
after World War II, defined full employment as more job openings than
people. Keynes offered different definitions at different times, and
left the impression that the definition would depend on
circumstances.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Followers of Keynes in the 1950s often
shifted the focus from employment, which can be defined in many ways, to
industrial production. So long as industrial production remained below
some specified number (such as 80%) they thought there was virtually no
chance of inflation, not even if the government injected a lot of new
money into the economy. All the new money would just raise industrial
production or employment, not prices.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Later, as the industrial sector of the
economy declined, Keynes-ians’s focus shifted from industrial capacity
to an economy wide “output gap,” defined as the difference between
actual gross domestic product and “potential” GDP. Using this concept,
inflation, if it came, would arrive after the output gap (and potential
employees) had all been utilized.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The trouble with all these concepts is
that neither idle capacity nor idle employees soak up newly printed
money. That money still creates inflation, whether of the visible or the
disguised kind. Inflation may help create an artificial boom, with
temporarily lower unemployment, but it will not be sustainable.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the 1960s and 1970s, Keynesians were
chagrined by the increasingly rampant inflation at a time when capacity
was not fully utilized. They were equally dumbfounded by the appearance
of stagflation, the combination of unemployment and inflation.</span>

#### 12a. Keynes: If necessary, inflation can be controlled by taxes.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the unlikely event that full
employment does arrive (accompanied by inflation), there are better
remedies than</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">clapping on a higher rate of
interest.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-33"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-33-back"><sup>33</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Instead of increasing interest rates,
government should raise taxes, run a budget surplus, and keep the extra
cash idle.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-34"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-34-back"><sup>34</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This will drain purchasing power out of
the economy and bring prices back down.\
</span>

#### 12b. Comment: The idea of governments successfully creating a budget surplus in order to combat inflation is completely fanciful.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Very high taxes actually make a budget
surplus harder to achieve, because they reduce economic output and thus
reduce government tax revenues. The Great Inflation of the 1970s again
provides some real life evidence.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The US government’s so-called policy mix
through most of the 1970s was completely Keynesian: low interest rates
(in relation to inflation) combined with very high tax rates (over 70%
at the top bracket). If Keynes was right, the high taxes should have
braked inflation, but instead it was spiraling out of control.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As previously noted, the new chairman of
the Federal Reserve, Paul Volcker, abandoned Keynesian orthodoxy in the
late 1970s by curtailing the printing of money. President Ronald Reagan
meanwhile persuaded Congress to reduce taxes dramatically, which further
broke the Keynesian mold. Most Keynesian economists warned that it would
not work. But the inflationary spiral subsided, the economy recovered,
and Reagan was reelected in a landslide.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The 1980s stand out as the decade when
the US, and also Britain, appeared to take a post-Keynesian course.
Policy changed in important respects. At the same time, key Keynesian
assumptions remained. The Federal Reserve, that is, government, still
controlled and managed interest rates. Governments still ran massive
budget deficits, even if the deficits were now driven by a combination
of tax cuts and government spending rather than by government spending
alone.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">By the end of the 1980s, conventional
Keynesians once again took command of the American economy. One of the
most prominent of them, Alan Greenspan, was appointed Federal Reserve
Chairman by Ronald Reagan himself.\
</span>

#### 13a. Keynes: High tax rates also contribute to a more just society.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Progressive income taxes, in which the
rich pay a higher and higher tax rate, help to reduce economic
inequality:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">\[An\] outstanding fault of the economic
society in which we live \[is\] its arbitrary and inequitable
distribution of wealth and
incomes.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-35"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-35-back"><sup>35</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes thought that high income and
death (estate) taxes had already done much to make a more economically
just society. But governments had hesitated to go further because they
were persuaded that the rich are best able to save, and that their
savings are needed to finance much-needed
investment.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-36"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-36-back"><sup>36</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes’s program of financing investment
with newly printed money injected into the banking system helps solve
this problem. Thanks to this innovation, society need no longer depend
on the savings of the rich.</span>

#### 13b. Comment: High tax rates are economically counterproductive.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We will not attempt to sort out the
equity versus inequity of wealth inequalities (see this author’s</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">Are the Rich
Necessary?</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">).</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">With regard to high taxes, as usual
Keynes does not tell us what “high” means. He seems to have said
different things about it at different times.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The heart of Keynes’s argument is that
newly printed money can take the place of traditional savings. We have
already seen that this is fallacious. Printing more and more money just
brings inflation or bubble followed by crash and slump, not perpetual
“semi-boom.” We need traditional savings. Without traditional savings,
there will be no real funds to invest. The savings of the rich are
especially important because they save the most. High tax rates are
inadvisable for this reason alone.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Yes, a country may be able to borrow
traditional savings from abroad. The United States did so for decades.
If the borrowed funds are put into productivity-enhancing investments
with a high return (not the case with the US), it may work out well, at
least for a time. But in the long run, no country can escape the need
for traditional savings, at least if it wishes to grow and
prosper.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">There are many other arguments against
high taxes: that they will destroy economic incentives, that they will
be evaded, that they will be wasted, that they will lead to government
corruption as tax relief is exchanged by politicians for campaign
contributions.</span><span class="calibre12 calibre3"><a href="#Chapter10.xhtml_ast1"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ast1-back"><sup>*</sup></a></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But, above all, there is the need for
savings. No government ever saves, so if we want savings, we had better
control taxes.</span>

#### 14a. Keynes: As important as low interest rates are, they may not be enough.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">If the government prints a great deal of
new money and injects it into the banking system, interest rates should
fall.</span><span class="calibre12 calibre3 calibre7 sgc-1"><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-24"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_nte-24-back"><sup>GG</sup></a></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But if they do not fall far enough,
other measures will be required to boost investment.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes thought it possible that cheap
new money from the government might not fully succeed in driving
interest rates down to the “optimum” low
level.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-37"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-37-back"><sup>37</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This is because “wealth-owners,” who
have not yet been completely displaced as lenders, might find low rates
“unacceptable” and succeed in blocking them. If so,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">the State, which is in a position to
calculate . . . on long views and on the basis of the general social
advantage . . . \[will have to\] directly organiz\[e\] . . .
investment.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-38"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-38-back"><sup>38</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The application of cheap loan rates
along with direct government investment is also the essential
prescription for handling an economic crash, an idea that will be
further developed in later chapters.\
</span>

#### 14b. Comment:

<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">To be discussed in
chapters 12, 13, and 15.\
</span>

#### 15a. Keynes: If low interest rates could be orchestrated at the global level, that would be even better.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">For now, national governments must take
the necessary actions to bring interest rates down and keep them low.
Eventually, global institutions might assist in this task.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes hoped that what became known as
the International Monetary Fund would instead be called a bank. He
further hoped that it would act as a global central bank with the power
to print new money and inject it into the global economic system in
order to reduce interest
rates.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-39"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-39-back"><sup>39</sup></a>

#### 15b. Comment:

<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">To be discussed in
chapter 17.\
</span>

#### 16a. Keynes: The need for lower interest rates is not a completely new idea.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We should pay our respects to the army
of heretics and cranks,</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-40"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-40-back"><sup>40</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">who in earlier periods argued for lower
interest rates.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Among these in particular are sixteenth
and seventeenth century Mercantilists whose doctrines contained
an</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">element of scientific
truth.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch10-41"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_ch10-41-back"><sup>41</sup></a>

#### 16b. Comment: There are several ironies here.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes is supposed to have invented a
“new economics.” He leads us to think so in the earlier chapters
of</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">. He then confusingly tells
us later in</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">The
General Theory</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">that he is really
reviving and refurbishing Mercantilist ideas from the 16th and 17th
centuries, ideas that economists over the years had come to dismiss as
gross fallacies. The truth is that Keynes’s ideas are neither new nor
correct.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is also ironic that Keynes, who liked
to number himself among the heretics and cranks, has now become so
deeply entrenched in the conventional wisdom of world governments. There
are those who think that, if alive today, he might have become a
Keynesian heretic, if only to keep fresh waters flowing. Perhaps. We
shall have more to say about that in chapter 18. But, for the moment, it
is Keynes’s critics who must face dismissal and scorn as “heretics and
cranks.”</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is always a perilous business to
point out that “the emperor has no clothes.” If one dares to look,
Keynes is an especially naked</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">figure, in the sense that his work is so
remarkably unsupported by evidence or logic. What he really offers (when
the opaquely jargonish arguments are finally parsed) is a kind of
shamanic utterance that we are supposed to take more or less on
faith.</span>

FOOTNOTE

<a href="#Chapter10.xhtml_ast1-back" id="Chapter10.xhtml_ast1">*</a> See
Note <a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-23"
id="Chapter10.xhtml_nte-23-back"><sup>FF</sup></a> and chapter 14

<span id="Chapter11.xhtml"></span>

## 11

## Spend More, Save Less, and Grow Poorer

#### 1a. Keynes: We need more consumption.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Consumption—to \[state\] the obvious—is
the sole . . . object of all economic
activity.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-1"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-1-back"><sup>1</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The “purposive” man is always trying to
secure a spurious and delusive immortality. . . . He does not love his
cat, but his cat’s kittens; nor, in truth, the kittens, but only the
kittens’ kittens, and so on forward forever to the end of cat-dom. For
him jam is not jam unless it is a case of jam tomorrow and never jam
today.</span> <a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-2"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-2-back"><sup>2</sup></a>

#### 1b. Comments:

##### i. Keynes did not practice what he preached.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Perhaps Keynes was preaching to himself.
He was certainly “purposive.” He was himself (dare we say it?) a saver.
His investment</span> capital did not have an immaculate conception—the
original stake was mostly saved prior to
1919.<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-3"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-3-back"><sup>3</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes pere did rescue his son at one
point from debts related to catastrophic investment losses, but did not
otherwise stake him.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">After Keynes had accumulated investment
capital, he did not spend it, but instead carefully nursed and tended it
until, after several major investment setbacks, he finally became rich.
When rich, he seems to have lived off his income, and did everything he
could to become richer. Moreover, Keynes was childless. Apart from
caring for his wife, he had no particular reason to want to die with a
large estate.\
</span>

##### ii. Keynes was not a vulgar Keynesian.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes was also a person of refined
tastes. If he were alive today, we can only imagine what he would have
thought of the vulgar Keynesianism that his ideas spawned, the
borrow-and-spend culture that not only abhors saving, but actually
approves of getting deeper and deeper in debt each year. Would he be
happy about financial companies routinely sending credit cards to
college students, the same students who are already in many cases
indentured to massive student loans provided directly or indirectly by
the government?</span> <a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-25"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_nte-25-back"><sup>HH</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">By 2005, half of Americans under age 35
had unpaid credit card balances averaging \$3,741, in addition to
educational, car, home, or other
debts.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-4"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-4-back"><sup>4</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">One critic has referred to this as “a
new form of feudalism,”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-5"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-5-back"><sup>5</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">with young debtors as chained to their
debts as serfs once were to their lord’s estate. Unfortunately young
people have no reason to regard this as abnormal. In many cases they
have grown up in households owning more cars than drivers (1.9 vehicles
per 1.75 drivers on average in America in
2008).</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-6"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-6-back"><sup>6</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Just as often, their parents have saved
nothing at all (zero average savings rate as of same date).</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We will recall that Keynes planned to
replace private savings with newly printed money injected into the
economy via the banking system. But he seemed to assume that this newly
printed money would be used either for business investment or government
public works projects such as roads. Even he might have blanched at the
prospect of consumers borrowing this ersatz money to finance their
lifestyle during the dot-com and housing bubbles.\
</span>

##### iii. Consumption is not the largest part of an economy.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Is consumption then the sole object of
all economic activity? No. It is one of several activities, each of
which must be in balance, both for the individual and for society as a
whole. Even US economic statistics, which have been constructed on
Keynesian principles, tend to mislead in this regard. We are told that
consumer spending represents 70% of the economy. But that is only
because all the business transactions—sales by one business to another
as the final product is assembled—have been netted out. If not
calculated in this way, production is a bigger part of the economy than
consumption.\
</span>

#### 2a. Keynes: The paradoxical parable of the cake that is baked but never eaten.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">19th century capitalists turned
self-denial and thrift into a kind of religion. But it was a religion
based on “bluff or deception.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-7"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-7-back"><sup>7</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">On the one hand the laboring classes
. . . could call their own very little of the cake that they and Nature
and the capitalists were cooperating to produce. And on the other hand
the capitalist classes were allowed to call the best part of the cake
theirs and were theoretically free to consume it, on the tacit
underlying condition that they consumed very little of it in practice.
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The duty of “saving” became nine-tenths
of virtue and the growth of the cake the object of true religion. . . .
And so the cake increased; but to what end was not clearly contemplated.
. . . Saving was for old age or for your children; but this was only in
theory—the virtue of the cake was that it was never to be consumed,
neither by you nor by your children after
you.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-8"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-8-back"><sup>8</sup></a></span>

#### 2b. Comments:

##### i. Applause (for the satire).

##### ii. Now the facts.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Living conditions for workers
significantly improved throughout the 18th and 19th centuries. Moreover,
the population doubled and then doubled again. Only a growing cake, made
possible by saving and investment, fed all those extra mouths. As Henry
Hazlitt has pointed out, many of us owe our very lives to the growing
cake, because population could not have grown so rapidly without
it.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A growing cake allows both owners and
workers to increase personal consumption as well as saving. Assume that
our incomes are growing at 3% a year, thanks to the investments we have
made from our savings. If we keep spending 80% and saving 20% year after
year, both the amount spent and the amount saved will double by the 25th
year.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Even if our only ambition is to spend
more, saving and investing still makes sense, because it will give us
more and more money to spend, and we will not have to wait 25 years to
feel the effect. Assume that my friend and I have an identical starting
income. My friend spends 100% of it. I save 20% and as a result my
income grows at 3%. Within only eight years, I will have more to spend
than my friend, and after that the gap will steadily grow.</span>

#### 3a. Keynes: The Cult of Saving

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The morals, the politics, the
literature, and the religion of the . . . \[19th century\] joined in a
grand conspiracy for the promotion of saving. God and Mammon were
reconciled. Peace on earth to men of good means. A rich man could, after
all, enter into the Kingdom of Heaven—if only he
saved.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-9"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-9-back"><sup>9</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The “classical” economists played a
pivotal role as secular priests propagating and safeguarding the dogma
of saving. Yes, they said, spending was needed to make an economy work.
If the baker buys fruits and vegetables from the greengrocer, the
greengrocer will then have money with which to buy bread from the baker.
But saving was another, much more special, form of spending. When we
save, the money does not simply disappear. It is spent on expanding
businesses, making them more efficient. If we work hard, produce, and
save, we will enjoy a cornucopia of cheaper, better, and more abundant
products. By this means, we will overcome unemployment, slump, and, in
the long run, poverty.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The Cult of Saving thus</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">conquered England as completely as the
Holy Inquisition conquered
Spain.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-10"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-10-back"><sup>10</sup></a>

#### 3b. Comments:

##### i. Applause.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">These little satirical burlesques are a
Keynesian specialty. First, the greedy capitalists (think of Dickens’s
Scrooge paradoxically redeemed not by the spirit of Christmas, but by
his own stinginess). Next the “classical” economists oppressing England
like hooded priests of the Inquisition. It is all good theater. Some
clarifications, however, are in order.</span>

##### ii. There were no “classical” economists.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The term “classical” economist is a
straw man, a caricature set up only to tear down. Economists from the
18th, 19th, and even 20th centuries cannot be lumped together as if they
speak with one voice.\
</span>

##### iii. The named economists have been misrepresented.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The named parties—David Ricardo, J. S.
Mill, J. B. Say, et al—did believe in hard work, production, and saving
as a reliable route to prosperity. But they did not suggest that those
three principles alone would bring us to an economic utopia free of
unemployment and slump. To minimize unemployment and slump, they would
have said, requires, in addition to hard work, production, and saving,
at the very least an unhampered price and profit system.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is often said that Keynes refuted
economists such as Ricardo, Mill, and Say. It is more accurate to say
that he deliberately mis-represented them and still failed to refute
them.\
</span>

##### iv. Say was right; Keynes wrong.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Consider, for example, Keynes’s alleged
refutation of Say’s Law. Say’s Law tells us that if society works and
produces, it will have the means to buy what it produces. This takes the
basic idea that the greengrocer, by selling fruits and vegetables, will
have the money to buy bread from the baker, and vice versa, and expands
it to society as a whole. By producing, society will earn money, and
that money will buy the production.</span><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-26"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_nte-26-back"><sup>II</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">What might be called Keynes’s Law, in
contrast to Say’s, may be stated as follows. If society spends, the
goods will be produced. This is also true, because production and
spending are really inseparable, just two sides of the same coin. But it
is nevertheless deeply misleading.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the circular flow of production and
spending, it is production that has pride of place. If we are
shipwrecked on a deserted island, having money to spend will not help at
all. If we do not produce, we will starve.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Even back in civilization, it is
producers who have a chance to grow rich. Spenders more often than not
end up poor. It is production that determines how much we can afford to
spend, not the reverse.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is true that we can spend more than
we produce by borrowing. But only for a time, not indefinitely. We may
also be able to increase our spending by running money off a printing
press in our basement. If we are a government, we will not be jailed for
this counterfeiting. But it will not make us richer in the long run,
because for society as a whole the extra money will just lead to
inflation of one kind or another.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Mill and Say were right; Keynes was
wrong. There is no substitute for hard work, production, and saving,
operating within an unhampered price and profit system. To argue
otherwise is to resort to sophistry. Moreover, this is dangerous
sophistry which impoverishes not only individuals, but entire
societies.\
</span>

#### 4a. Keynes: The trouble with savings is that they are often not invested. Lying fallow, they reduce demand. As demand falls, so does the economy.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">If societies produce and save and the
savings are smoothly and fully invested, all will be well. But that is
the rub—there is no certainty whatever that savings will be
invested.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Perhaps the savers will want too high a
rate of interest, higher than the business owners can
pay.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-11"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-11-back"><sup>11</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Perhaps business owners or managers are
lacking in the confidence or conviction required to borrow and invest.
Perhaps the saver will simply decide not to lend—to hoard cash
instead.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We should keep in mind that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">an act of individual saving means—so to
speak—a decision not to have dinner today. But it does not necessitate a
decision \[either\] to have dinner a week hence or a year hence \[or to
invest what is not spent\].</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-12"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-12-back"><sup>12</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">“absurd”</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">to think that investment will be
increased by one pound or one dollar for every pound or dollar that is
not spent on consumption.</span><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-27"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_nte-27-back"><sup>JJ</sup></a>

#### 4b. Comments:

##### i. Savings do not ordinarily go unused.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The picture of savers zealously skipping
dinner, not to lose a pound of flesh, but to gain a pound of money, is
another burlesque. Savers do not customarily skip dinner.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">What about the other assertions? Do
savings often sit idle, clogging up the economic system, reducing the
Demand that allegedly drives economic growth? There is no reason to
expect this to happen. People generally save in order to earn money on
the savings. They do this by investing it. If savings are converted into
investments, the money will be spent on business expansion—on new
employees, new equipment, new facilities—and thus will flow back into
the economy. As it flows back into the economy, it can be used to buy
the products that investment brings us. This in turn will bring
employment, profits, and more savings. There is no reason why this
circular flow cannot continue indefinitely.\
</span>

##### ii. Savers do not control interest rates.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes responds that savers often want
too high a rate of interest, higher than business owners can pay. As we
discussed in the last</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">chapter,
this assumes a fanciful one-sided market. A free money market is
two-sided, and it “solves” for the interest rate that will ensure
investment of all available funds.\
</span>

##### iii. If savers lack the confidence to invest, we must ask why.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes also says that business owners or
investors may lack the confidence necessary to borrow and invest. This
certainly happens during an economic crisis, but there is a good reason
for it then. It means that some element of the price and profit system
is temporarily deranged, and savers must be very careful not to lose
their savings. For example, consumer prices may be rising (inflation) or
falling too fast (severe deflation), or asset prices may be
collapsing.</span><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-28"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_nte-28-back"><sup>KK</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Ironically, the most likely source of a
destabilizing inflation is the same new money that Keynes wants
government to print. The most likely source of severe deflation is the
inflation, visible or disguised, which typically precedes it. In either
case, the problem is not uninvested savings; they are just symptomatic
of a larger problem. Under these circumstances, Keynesian inflationary
policies will just make the problem worse.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Finally Keynes says that savers may
simply decide not to invest, to hoard cash instead. Well, everyone does
need a bit of idle cash. We keep a few dollar bills in our wallet for
emergencies. Lacking an emergency, this cash may never be spent.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But we do not ordinarily keep cash under
the proverbial mattress or in a lockbox. We invest it. If we do not
invest, it is not because of some atavistic need to hoard. It is almost
always because we are worried and do not want to waste our precious
capital.</span><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-29"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_nte-29-back"><sup>LL</sup></a>

##### iv. John Stuart Mill refutes Keynes.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">During the 19th century, economics in
England might be said to have been dominated by John Stuart Mill
(1806–1873), one</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">of the
“classical” economists criticized by Keynes. His economic
textbook,</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">Principles of Political
Economy</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, was published in 1848
and reigned supreme in England and America for half a century.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Hazlitt found a Mill essay in which he
specifically addresses the Keynesian idea that savings might accumulate,
unused, and clog up the economic system. In this essay, Mill concludes
that it is a</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">“palpable absurdity.”</span>
<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-13"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-13-back"><sup>13</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Why? Because, as we already noted, the
lure of a return, of profit, ensures that savings are invested and flow
into the spending stream. Unlike consumer spending, investment spending
helps production grow, and thus leads to more income, and more consumer
spending, and more saving down the road. There is only one caveat: to
make this happen, prices (including wages) must be unhampered. The
profit system must be respected.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This does not mean, per Mills, that no
savings, no capital, are ever “idle.” We may even have “a large
proportion of capital . . .
idle.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-14"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-14-back"><sup>14</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This is because the economy is always
changing. Demand is increasing here, decreasing there. Some businesses
will be contracting, some expanding. There will inevitably be some idle
capital and some related unemployment as a result of these changes. This
is not to be regretted. It is the price of economic advance. Without
these changes, dislocations, and reallocations of capital, we would
still be living in caves.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Whenever we see all capital employed,
almost all businesses thriving and expanding, this should serve as a
warning. We are not seeing an economic advance; we are seeing a bubble.
It means, as Mill said, that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">some general delusion is
afloat.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-15"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-15-back"><sup>15</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is not that Mill is an old fogy, that
he is only happy when large numbers of people are out of work. If wages
are allowed to</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">float freely with
other prices, almost everyone who wants a job should be able to find one
without a long wait. But there must be at least frictional unemployment,
people between jobs, because that is how an economy changes. To aim for
full employment and no idle resources, as Keynes does, is to aim for a
bubble, and bubbles will always blow up, bringing depression and deep
unemployment in their wake. As Mill concludes,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">When the delusion vanishes, those whose
commodities are relatively in excess must diminish their production or
be ruined: and if during the high prices they have built mills and
erected machinery, they will be likely to repent at
leisure.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-16"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-16-back"><sup>16</sup></a>

#### 5a. Keynes: The richer we become, the more we are menaced by unused savings.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We should not deceive ourselves that a
mismatch between Saving and investment (too much savings, too little
investment) is either a rare or an unlikely occurrence. On the
contrary:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">There has been a chronic tendency
throughout human history \[for savings to exceed
investment\].</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-17"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-17-back"><sup>17</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">And it has become more of a problem, not
less of one, as societies have advanced.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We all know that becoming richer makes
it easier to save. In the same way, a rich society tends to save more
than a poor society. As the savings increase, so does the mismatch
between savings and investment.</span>

#### 5b. Comment: If we have too much unused savings, why keep printing more money?

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes offers not a shred of evidence
that savings have exceeded investment throughout human history. This is
another of his hunches.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">He is correct that rich societies, like
rich individuals, save more. But it does not follow that they produce
more “excess” or “unused” savings.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is interesting that, more than half a
century after Keynes’s death, the assertion that we are still suffering
from a savings glut continues to be made with little or no
substantiation. For example, Ben Bernanke, chairman of the US Federal
Reserve, Alan Greenspan, chairman of the US Federal Reserve prior to
Bernanke, and Paul Krugman, Nobel Prize winning economist, have all
claimed that the world economy since 2002 has been plagued by “excess”
savings, much of it coming from
Asia.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-18"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-18-back"><sup>18</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">How these three would know this is hard
to say, especially since the world is also awash in ersatz, government
printed money, some of it supplied by Greenspan and Bernanke. This is
the same substance that Keynes claimed was as “genuine” as real
savings.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-19"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-19-back"><sup>19</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">If the high savings rate in Asia really
has created a savings glut, then why have the world’s central banks,
including Asian central banks, printed money at such a clip? Why do they
not stop, so that the real savings can be used up?\
</span>

#### 6a. Keynes: The Paradox of Thrift

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">During an economic slump, we especially
want to save more, because we fear losing our jobs. This just makes
things worse, because the additional savings are unlikely to find an
outlet in investment at that moment. The unused savings sit idle, the
flow of money through the economy slows further and the slump deepens.
What we need to understand, under these circumstances, is that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">the more virtuous we are, the more
determinedly thrifty, the more obstinately orthodox . . . the more our
incomes will have to fall. . . . Obstinacy can bring only penalty and no
reward. The result is
inevitable.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-20"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-20-back"><sup>20</sup></a>

#### 6b. Comments:

##### i. There is no Paradox of Thrift.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We touched on this issue in chapter 1.
The Keynesian “paradox of thrift,” (along with the related “fallacy of
composition,” the idea that behavior advisable for an individual may be
inadvisable for the larger community) was intoned by virtually all
commentators during the Crash of 2008. Yes, they said, the American
people had spent too much, had borrowed too much, had saved too little
for many years. God lead us back to virtue, but not yet, to paraphrase
St. Augustine. We must realize that too quick a return to virtue, too
quick a turn away from borrowing and spending would just plunge us
further into misery.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">There is no paradox here; Keynes is
wrong. It is prudent for families facing job loss to try to put
something away. It is also prudent for a society that has overspent and
overborrowed to start saving. This is true with or without an economic
slump.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As we have discussed, the slump came
because the government (or governments) artificially stimulated the
economy by printing new money and injecting it into the economy through
the financial system. This lowered interest rates and encouraged a wave
of wasteful borrowing and spending by both businesses and consumers. In
particular, vast sums were borrowed which could never be paid back,
either because they were simply consumed, or because they were invested
in poorly chosen projects.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Under these circumstances, consuming
more alcohol will not cure the hangover. The bad investments of the
recent past need</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">to be
liquidated, or at least marked down in price. Until this happens, savers
should build their cash positions and refuse to use them. To invest at
the old, unrealistic asset prices would just continue the old pattern of
throwing money away.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Once liquidation has been accomplished
and lower asset prices prevail, the more saving the better. These
savings will bring down interest rates naturally and provide funds to
rebuild the economy on the ruins of the past.\
</span>

##### ii. Profits are the key to recovery.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynesian analysis tells us that what is
lacking during a depression is demand. Since private savings (in this
view) will lie fallow, will not be invested, the only effective demand
comes from consumer or government spending. But what really drives an
economy is not demand; it is production. And what really drives
production is profits. If we want to restore the economy, we need to
restore profits, genuine profits, not the phony profits of a
bubble.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A collapse of profits tells us that the
price and profit system of the market has been damaged, usually by
government interventions to reduce interest rates, increase wages,
increase consumption, subsidize some sectors and enforce cartels in
others. It is not the savers who have wrecked the economy, it is
government interventions that have penalized savers and ultimately
destroyed profits.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Even Keynes must have known how
important profits are. In his</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">Treatise on
Money</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, he acknowledged
that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">the engine which drives enterprise is
. . . profit.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-21"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-21-back"><sup>21</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">By the time he wrote</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, Keynes often used
jargonish circumlocutions to sidestep the word profit, terms such as
“the marginal efficiency of capital.” But the inescapable truth is that
profit is the key to prosperity. And the way to rebuild genuine
profit</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">is to allow all prices,
including interest rates and currencies, to tell the truth about the
economy. In an environment of free prices, hard work, production, and
saving will do all that is required, just as John Stuart Mill said they
would almost two hundred years ago.\
</span>

#### 7a. Keynes: there are better and worse ways to address the problem of a savings glut.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Beginning with some of the worse ways,
we could hope for</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">\[enough\] unemployment to keep us . . .
sufficiently . . . poor . . . and \[our\] standard of life sufficiently
miserable to bring savings \[down\].
. . .</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-22"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-22-back"><sup>22</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We could instead hope that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">millionaires \[will stop their
relentless saving and instead\] find their satisfaction in \[putting
their savings to use by\] building mighty mansions to contain their
bodies when alive and pyramids to shelter them after death, or,
repenting of their sins, erect cathedrals and endow monasteries.
. . .</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-23"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-23-back"><sup>23</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We could rely on the unexpected:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">\[Throughout history, natural disasters
such as\] earthquakes, even wars . . . \[have\] serve\[d\] to increase
wealth \[by using up savings\].
. . .</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-24"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-24-back"><sup>24</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We might petition governments, even
those wholly devoted to free market (“laissez-faire”) principles
to</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">fill old bottles with bank notes, bury
them at suitable depths in disused coal mines which are then filled
. . . with town rubbish, and leave it to private enterprise . . . to
\[invest in\] dig\[ging\] the notes up
again.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-25"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-25-back"><sup>25</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As Keynes says, it would be</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">more sensible to build houses . . . but
digging up bank notes is not so different from “gold mining” and would
equally serve as a way to consume excessive savings.</span>
<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-26"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-26-back"><sup>26</sup></a>

#### 7b. Comments:

##### i Applause.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">These suggestions are all satirical
gems.\
</span>

##### ii. Wars, natural disasters, and “make work” do not create wealth.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Wars and natural disasters can only use
up wealth, they cannot increase it. Investment after World War II may
have boomed to meet “pent-up” consumer demand. But that did not make us
richer than we would have been without the war.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The comparison of buried paper money to
gold is rather a joke on Keynes. His advice to print more and more money
has, over the years, resulted in a rapidly depreciating currency. It
will be recalled that the dollar, since the formation of the Federal
Reserve in 1913, has lost over 95% of its purchasing power. By contrast,
gold has kept its purchasing power, and become an investment refuge for
those who wonder what governments, inspired by Keynes, will do
next.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It could also be argued that governments
have repeatedly followed Keynes’s tongue-in-cheek advice to pour money
down a hole in the ground, and that the amount of money wasted in this
way, if properly invested, might by now have pulled the entire world out
of poverty.</span>

#### 8a. Keynes: There are, of course, better ways to reduce unused savings.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Reducing interest rates in order to
attract more borrowing, thereby reducing the glut of savings, is the
best way. But there are alternatives. One may</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">consume . . . more,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">that is, spend more as consumers so that
we save less, or</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">work . . . less,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">that is, reduce our income and thus our
ability to save.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes said that these latter two
methods serve</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">“just as well” as more
investment.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-27"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-27-back"><sup>27</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A practical way for society to consume
more is to tax the rich at high rates and redistribute the wealth to
those who are needy and thus sure to spend
it.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-28"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-28-back"><sup>28</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">If we keep clearly in mind that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">the growth in wealth, so far from being
dependent on the abstinence \[savings\] of the rich, as is commonly
supposed, is more likely to be impeded by
it,</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-29"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-29-back"><sup>29</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">we will then see that “death duties”
(estate taxes) as well as progressive income tax rates will help society
prosper.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">What if the rich succeed politically in
blocking these death duties and high income taxes? If so, governments
can also borrow from the rich. This will soak up their excess savings.
And, having borrowed the money, government can then spend it, which will
get it into circulation and stimulate the economy. In this case,
government becomes what might be called the spender of last
resort.</span>

#### 8b. Comments:

##### i. The more you spend, the more you have.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the passages above, Keynes makes
explicit what was implicit before. He wants to see more spending and
does not really care if the spending takes the form of consumption
rather than investment. Consumption works “just as well.” Spending is
the way to wealth, saving the way to poverty.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Henry Hazlitt says about these ideas:
“How marvelous is the Keynesian world! The more you spend the more you
\[have\]. The more you eat your cake, the more cake \[to
eat\].”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-30"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-30-back"><sup>30</sup></a>

##### ii. Estate taxes cannot make us richer.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">According to Keynes, conventional views
about death duties (that they reduce the invested wealth of a country)
are</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">confused.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-31"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-31-back"><sup>31</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">He agrees that using death duties to
reduce other taxes will lead to more consumption. But since consumption
increases national income, and increased national income leads to more
investment as well as consumption, death duties will actually increase
investment, not reduce it.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This is so ludicrous that it hardly
seems worth discussing. Consider: estates are almost always fully
invested. Keynes tells us that by liquidating the investment, and
spending it all, we will magically get even more investment in the end.
If so, then why not liquidate all of our investments, spend everything
we can get our hands on, and then confidently await the cornucopia of
wealth to follow?</span>

##### iii. We need workers and savers, including rich workers and savers.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes’s is indeed a fantasy world, one
which unfortunately we all have to live in because of the dominance of
his ideas among world governments. The truth is that an economy can only
thrive with private savings. At the moment, the rich, precisely because
they have so much, are the most reliable source of these savings.
Therefore, at the moment, we need the rich both to supply the needed
investment funds and to guide the investments.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Are there ways to make us less dependent
on the rich? Yes. Some of them are explored in the last chapter of this
author’s</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">Are the
Rich Necessary?</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">But, in the
meantime, transferring money from the rich, the most reliable savers, to
government, the inveterate spendthrift, will just impoverish
everybody.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As writer, lawyer, economist, and actor
Ben Stein has written, under the heading: “How to Ruin American
Enterprise”:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Sneer at hard work and thrift. . . .
Leave the plodding . . . worker and saver in the dust. . . . Enact a tax
system that encourages class antagonism and punishes saving, while
rewarding indebtedness, frivolity, and consumption. Tax the fruits of
labor many times: . . . as income\[,\] . . . as real . . . property\[,\]
. . . as capital gains\[,\] . . . again, at a staggeringly high level,
at death. . . . This will deprive us of much needed capital for new
investment, for innovation and our own personal
aspirations.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch11-32"
id="Chapter11.xhtml_ch11-32-back"><sup>32</sup></a>

##### iv. Summary

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes told us that we suffer from
excess savings. He would paradoxically solve the problem by introducing
a new kind of saving (new money created by the government) that would be
just</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">as “genuine” as private
savings. This new money would not cause inflation. All of this has
turned out to be wrong:</span>

![](Images/chpt_fig_001.png)  <span class="calibre6 calibre3">We have
too little saving, at least in the US.</span>

![](Images/chpt_fig_001.png)  <span class="calibre6 calibre3">Money
newly printed by government is not, by any stretch of the imagination,
“genuine” savings.</span>

![](Images/chpt_fig_001.png)  <span class="calibre6 calibre3">The new
money, introduced through the banking system in the form of very cheap
credit, does create inflation.</span>

![](Images/chpt_fig_001.png)  <span class="calibre6 calibre3">If the new
money primarily flows into consumer goods, it produces a conventional
inflation. If it primarily flows into investment assets, it produces an
investment bubble like the 1920s or the recent dot-com and house
bubbles.</span>

![](Images/chpt_fig_001.png)  <span class="calibre6 calibre3">Either
way, Keynes’s print, lend, spend, and tax polices consume and destroy
the private savings on which we depend for a prosperous economy.</span>

<span id="Chapter12.xhtml"></span>

## 12

## What (Not) to Do about Wall Street

#### 1a. Keynes: Because private market participants do not know what they are doing, they lack confidence.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">So far, lower interest rates are the
medicine prescribed by Dr. Keynes. But this prescription will not by
itself guarantee more investment in a market system. When business
owners and managers consider a new investment, perhaps a new facility,
borrowing costs are important, but not as important as
expectations.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Investor expectations in turn depend
heavily on a purely psychological factor, the state of business
confidence. Unfortunately business confidence is generally weak.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The average person thinks that business
owners and managers know what they are doing and, in particular, know a
great deal about the future return of a factory, a mine, a product, or a
service. But this is not so. All human beings, even so-called experts,
are mostly in the dark about the future. Their knowledge of the
future</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">amounts to little and sometimes to
nothing,</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-1"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-1-back"><sup>1</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">and their ability to make accurate,
especially pinpoint, forecasts is usually nil.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">People in general and business investors
in particular cope with their ignorance of (and anxiety about) the
future by falling back on a simple convention. They assume that what has
happened in the recent past will continue to happen.</span>
<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-2"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-2-back"><sup>2</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Unfortunately this device is</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">arbitrary . . . weak . . . \[and\]
precarious.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-3"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-3-back"><sup>3</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It often fails, and failure brings with
it a psychological shock.\
</span>

#### 1b. Comment: Business owners and managers do not think the way Keynes says they think.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Business is about people, not forecasts.
The first requirement is to know your customers and their needs
extremely well. The next step is to try to meet those needs in an
effective (and also cost effective) way. It is rarely, if ever, a
question of making a pinpoint forecast of the future rate of return on a
plant or even a product and then comparing this forecasted rate of
return with interest rates.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes is correct that people very often
try to drive by looking in the rear view mirror. Psychological studies
suggest that this is an ingrained human trait. But good business
operators consider a variety of possible future conditions or outcomes.
They try to position themselves so that, even if the worst outcome
arrives, they will at least avoid bankruptcy.\
</span>

#### 2a. Keynes: The role of “animal spirits.”

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Even if some business owners or managers
guess right about the future, many will not. It is “probable” that
business returns on average “disappoint,”</span>
<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-4"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-4-back"><sup>4</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">especially in relation to the “hopes”
which precede them.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-5"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-5-back"><sup>5</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Why then do business investors keep
wanting to</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">play the “game”? Not
presumably out of “cold calculation,” but rather out of</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">animal
spirits.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-6"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-6-back"><sup>6</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Unfortunately “animal spirits” depend
on</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">the nerves and hysteria and even the
digestions \[of the players\].</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-7"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-7-back"><sup>7</sup></a>

#### 2b. Comment: This Is another Keynesian burlesque.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It cannot possibly be “probable” that
business returns on average are disappointing. How can anyone know? No
standard of success is even suggested, other than prior “hopes,” and no
evidence for anything.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Is business a game? Well, yes, in part.
But the desire to earn a living, support oneself and one’s family, build
a productive enterprise, and so forth would seem to be larger motives
than simply expressing one’s animal spirits. If business, in the end, is
merely a matter of “digestion,” can the same perhaps be said about
economic policy eructation from a Cambridge don?\
</span>

#### 3a. Keynes: What’s wrong with the stock market, part one.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This is already a weak foundation on
which to build a modern economy. But it is made even weaker by the
pernicious influence of the stock market.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The stock market is not all bad. It is a
way to finance companies. By offering “liquidity,” the ability to buy
and sell investments, such markets may also persuade the timid investor
to pull money out from under the mattress and actually make an
investment. Of course the liquidity is largely illusory. People cannot
all get in or get out at the same time, which they typically want to
do.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Then too, if money is invested in
existing stocks, it will not be invested in new plant, equipment,
employees, products, and services, which is what real investment is
about. Much stock market investment is really sterile, not much better
for society than keeping the money under a
mattress.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-8"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-8-back"><sup>8</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The pricing of shares on a public market
is often “absurd.”</span> <a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-9"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-9-back"><sup>9</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes has heard that the shares of ice
companies sell at a higher price in the summer when profits are
seasonally high than in winter. A rational market would know
better.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">If the stock market rises, this has the
beneficial effect of boosting business confidence. But a fall depresses
it. A fall may also depress consumer demand, because consumers who have
invested will feel less rich, and even those who have not invested will
fear for their jobs.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-10"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-10-back"><sup>10</sup></a>

#### 3b. Comment: One fallacy and two misstatements.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As previously touched on in Note EE, it
is fallacious to regard the purchase of an existing share of stock as
sterile (because it does not flow directly to the company and therefore
will not be used directly for business expansion). Since there is always
a buyer and a seller for every market transaction, my purchase of a
share transfers cash to the seller. The seller in turn may spend or
invest it, but one way or another, the cash will find a place in the
economy’s circular flow. Moreover if more people buy a company’s
existing shares, the price will rise. This means that when the company
issues new shares, it will have a lower cost of capital, and thus find
it cheaper to expand.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes’s rather cavalier treatment of
fact is illustrated by his reference to ice company shares. He tells us
“it is said” that ice company shares sell for more in summer than in
winter. Ice companies of course no longer exist, but Henry Hazlitt
checked the historical record and found the statement to be
false.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-11"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-11-back"><sup>11</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">By the way, did you notice the
outlandish definition of liquidity that Keynes slipped in? According to
him, a market must be able to absorb the simultaneous purchase or
(conversely) simultaneous sale of all shares in order to be considered
truly liquid.\
</span>

#### 4a. Keynes: What’s wrong with the stock market, part two.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The worst aspect of the stock market is
its</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">“casino”
atmosphere.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-12"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-12-back"><sup>12</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The ostensible purpose of a stock market
is to channel private savings into the most socially useful (and thus
profitable) investments.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-13"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-13-back"><sup>13</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This requires a “long-term” point of
view.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-14"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-14-back"><sup>14</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The “best brains of Wall Street,”
however, are completely unconcerned with the long-term. They are not
even concerned with learning very much about the companies they
buy.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-15"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-15-back"><sup>15</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Their “game,” which they play with the
utmost “zest” is to identify those stocks which will become popular and
to buy them first.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-16"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-16-back"><sup>16</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Since everyone else is playing the same
game, this means in effect</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">anticipating what the average opinion
\[will\] expect . . . the average opinion to be,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">and then profiting from a correct
guess.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-17"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-17-back"><sup>17</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Under these circumstances, genuine
long-term investing becomes “so difficult . . . as to be scarcely
practicable” and anyone who attempts it will, paradoxically, seem
“unconventional” and therefore
“rash.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-18"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-18-back"><sup>18</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Even the most conservative investment
committees will be uncomfortable with a long-term approach
because</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">worldly wisdom teaches that it is better
for reputations to fail conventionally than to succeed
unconventionally.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-19"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-19-back"><sup>19</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Wall Street might seem to be merely a
“spectacle,” but its dis-functionality has serious consequences:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Speculators may do no harm as bubbles on
a steady stream of enterprise. But the position is serious when
enterprise becomes the bubble on a whirlpool of
speculation.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-20"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-20-back"><sup>20</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Under these circumstances, capital will
not only be misallocated. Average citizens will also lose faith in the
system.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is one thing to watch some people get
unimaginably rich, if extremes of wealth are thought to reflect hard
work, astute judgment, and the production of vital goods for society as
a whole. Even then, the winners are getting far too much, whether from
the point of view of justice or from the point of view of providing
useful incentives.</span> <a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-21"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-21-back"><sup>21</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But it is quite another thing if vast
rewards go not to the disciplined and deserving, but only to lucky
gamblers. In time, the social system will unravel, no one will want to
work, and everyone will want to gamble.</span>
<a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-22"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-22-back"><sup>22</sup></a>

#### <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre7 sgc-1"><span id="Chapter12.xhtml_t145" class="calibre4">4b. Comments:</span>\
</span>

##### i. Keynes is describing a “bubble” market.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes vividly portrays the stock market
as a gambler’s den. It articulates what many have vaguely felt. But is
it accurate?</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is a reasonably accurate description
of stock markets during a bubble, during periods such as the 1920s,
1990s, or the 2000s. It is during bubbles that stock buyers are most
likely to focus on “anticipating . . . the average opinion” rather than
on company fundamentals. For Keynes to describe out-of-control bubble
markets as normal, and then condemn them, is of course more than ironic,
because it is precisely the print-lend-and-spend government policies
urged by Keynes that inflate these bubbles in the first place.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">To the untrained eye, bubble markets are
about private greed, not about government missteps.</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">The</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">Economist</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">magazine tells us that:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">When people look back on a bubble, they
tend to blame the mess on crookedness, greed, and the collective
insanity of others. What else but madness could explain all these
overpriced Dutch tulips?</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-23"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-23-back"><sup>23</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But bubble markets are not just
manifestations of private greed or madness. As Peter R. Fisher, former
undersecretary of the US Treasury and New York Federal Reserve Bank
official, explains:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Capitalism is premised on the idea that
capital is a scarce commodity, and we are going to ration it with a
price mechanism. When you make short term funds \[available\]
essentially free with negative real rates \[rates lower than inflation,
as happened for example 2001– 2004\], crazy things start to
happen.</span> <a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-30"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_nte-30-back"><sup>MM</sup></a>
<sup><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-24"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-24-back">24</a></sup>

##### ii. More logical problems.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">There are many oddities about Keynes’s
account of Wall Street excesses in</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">. For example, he
disparages stock investors as speculators, yet also disparages people
who hold cash (in preference to stocks) as speculators. These people are
allegedly operating from a</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">speculative motive</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">because they think they</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">know . . . better than the market what
the market will bring forth.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-25"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-25-back"><sup>25</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Does this mean, then, that both the
avoidance and the taking of risk make us speculators?</span>
<span class="calibre12 calibre3 calibre7 sgc-1"><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-31"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_nte-31-back"><sup>NN</sup></a></span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Another oddity: Keynes, who famously
said that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the long run we are all dead,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">who recommended more spending, less
saving, more concern with the present, less with the future, now tells
us that short-term investing is very bad. This is indeed confusing.
Which Keynes are we supposed to listen to?</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">By the way, we should not believe Keynes
when he says that rampant short-term investing makes long-term
investing</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">so difficult as to be scarcely
practicable.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-26"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-26-back"><sup>26</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This is completely illogical. In the
investment world, when most people are looking solely for short-term
opportunities, that makes long-term investing easier, not more
difficult. Why? Because there is less competition for good long-term
investment ideas.\
</span>

##### iii. More inconsistency.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">What is perhaps oddest of all about
Keynes’s disparagement of speculation is that he himself was such an
active speculator. He took concentrated positions in stocks,
commodities, and foreign exchange; was extremely active (turning over
his stocks 100% a year on average); and leveraged his positions with
borrowed money (frequently more than one pound borrowed for each pound
of his own money).</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-27"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-27-back"><sup>27</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In 1920, this aggressive stance
temporarily bankrupted him, but he bounced back with the help of loans
from family and friends. From 1922–1929, he seems only to have been
moderately successful, trailing the</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-2">Banker’s Magazine</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">stock index in five years out of seven.
He failed to anticipate trouble at the end of the 1920s and primarily
because of his leverage lost 86% of his capital. He was again caught
short in 1937, lost half his capital, but succeeded in recouping most of
the 1937 losses by the time of his
death.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-28"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-28-back"><sup>28</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">By 1938, speaking to the investment
committee of King’s College, Cambridge, he said that he no longer
believed in active buying and selling, but had instead adopted a
strategy of buying-and-</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">holding a
fairly small number of concentrated positions. He praised
diversification among different asset classes. Most ironically of all,
given his sermons against the use of gold as money, he specifically
recommended the inclusion of gold mining shares in any portfolio for
diversification purposes because of their lack of correlation with
common stocks.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-29"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-29-back"><sup>29</sup></a>

#### 5a. Keynes: The best remedy for stock market failure is for government to direct investment itself.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As we have seen, the problem of
converting savings into investment has two components. The first
obstacle is that interest rates tend to be chronically too high, which
discourages investment, and leaves large amounts of savings unused.
Government can alleviate this by printing more money and injecting it
into the banking system to bring interest rates down.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">But this solves only half the problem.
The other problem is that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">the psychology of \[private investors,
both in business and on Wall Street or other stock markets, is\]
disobedient . . . and . . .
uncontrollable.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch12-30"
id="Chapter12.xhtml_ch12-30-back"><sup>30</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Operating in a cloud of ignorance,
private investors tend to manic highs and morose lows. When morose they
cannot be prodded to take advantage of even the most attractive interest
rates.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The entire system of private investment
is not</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">intelligent . . . \[,\] is not virtuous
