the end of World War II:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes was in his most lucid and
persuasive mood; and the effect was irresistible. At such moments, I
often find myself thinking that Keynes must be one of the most
remarkable men that have ever lived—the quick logic, the birdlike swoop
of intuition, the vivid fancy, the wide vision, above all the
incomparable sense of the fitness of words, all combine to make
something several degrees beyond the limit of ordinary human
achievement.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch19-15"
id="Chapter19.xhtml_ch19-15-back"><sup>15</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As noted, however, not everyone was an
admirer. Henry Morgenthau, US secretary of the Treasury, wrote in his
diary that “\[Keynes is\] one of the fellows that just knows all the
answers.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch19-16"
id="Chapter19.xhtml_ch19-16-back"><sup>16</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Fred Vinson, Morgenthau’s successor and
later Chief Justice of the Supreme Court, also failed to appreciate
Keynes’s style. When the two were together at Bretton Woods in New
Hampshire to complete work on a new global monetary system, Keynes
playfully referred to the Sleeping Beauty Ballet by expressing, in an
after dinner toast, the hope that no malicious fairy, no Carabosse,
would spoil their work. Vinson privately quipped that “I don’t mind
being called malicious, but I do mind being called a
fairy.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch19-17"
id="Chapter19.xhtml_ch19-17-back"><sup>17</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter19.xhtml_t23"
class="calibre4">Keynes had an especially rocky relationship with Harry
Dex</span>ter White, the US Treasury representative who, more than any
other individual, fashioned the post-war monetary system (and</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter19.xhtml_t24"
class="calibre4">who later turned out to be a Soviet sympathizer and
sometime</span> informant). James Meade described the interaction of the
two “prima donnas”:</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch19-18"
id="Chapter19.xhtml_ch19-18-back"><sup>18</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes and White sit next to each
other . . . without any agenda . . . they go for each other in a
strident duet of discord which after a crescendo of abuse on either side
leads up to a chaotic
adjournment.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch19-19"
id="Chapter19.xhtml_ch19-19-back"><sup>19</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">White had the upper hand, because he
represented the dominant power, and the treaty ultimately reflected his
thinking more than Keynes’s. But Keynes still dominated the debate, as
he always did. This initially frustrated White, then made him anxious
and physically sick, and finally led him to send his deputy to the
meetings.</span> <a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch19-20"
id="Chapter19.xhtml_ch19-20-back"><sup>20</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes’s masterful biographer Robert
Skidelsky suggests that his subject’s “true greatness” lay in rhetoric,
“using that word in the classic sense of the ‘art of persuasion.’
”</span> <a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch19-21"
id="Chapter19.xhtml_ch19-21-back"><sup>21</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This seems just, especially when applied
to Keynes speaking, and especially when applied to Keynes speaking
extemporaneously.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter19.xhtml_t25"
class="calibre4">Keynes was of course more than a speaker. He was a
writer as well,</span> and here the record is mixed, as we shall discuss
in the next chapter.</span>

<span id="Chapter20.xhtml"></span>

## 20

## Keynes Writing

**<span class="dropcap">I</span><span class="small">T WAS
KEYNES’S</span>** <span class="calibre6 calibre3">second book,</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The Economic Consequences
of the Peace</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">(1920), that made
him world famous and launched his career as a public intellectual. His
thesis, that the victors of World War I had sowed the seeds of future
conflict by treating Germany harshly, remains debatable. Historian Paul
Johnson calls it “one of the most destructive books of the \[twentieth\]
century,”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-1"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-1-back"><sup>1</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t27"
class="calibre4">because it encouraged acquiescence in German military
revival.</span></span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Whatever one thinks of the thesis, the
style is clear, sparkling, clever, and witty, although a bit mannered
for modern tastes. Keynes never wrote so well again, perhaps because his
schedule was always jam-packed and he simply lacked the time to
concentrate. Another book almost as good,</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">Essays in
Persuasion</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, repackaged material
from</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The Economic
Consequences of the Peace</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">along
with shorter pieces originally written for periodicals or newspapers or
as pamphlets.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t28"
class="calibre4">We have already mentioned (in chapter 4) Keynes’s
excellent but</span> highly technical</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">Treatise on
Probability</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">(1921). The two
volume</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t29"
class="calibre4">Treatise on Money</span></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">(1930) was meant to be a magnum opus,
but fell short even in Keynes’s view. He wrote his mother that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t30"
class="calibre4">artistically it is a failure—I have changed my mind
too</span> much during the course of \[writing\]
it.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-2"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-2-back"><sup>2</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t31"
class="calibre4">Biographer Robert Skidelsky notes the “dreadfully
convoluted</span> sentences” and “dismal algebra” in part of volume one,
although he feels that some of the “sparkle” returns in volume
two.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-3"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-3-back"><sup>3</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In his preface to</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">(1936), Keynes tells us that the book is
for professional economists. He must nevertheless have entertained hopes
for a wider audience, because he kept the price down to the then unheard
of sum of five shillings (\$1.50 in the
US).</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-4"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-4-back"><sup>4</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">With the exception of a few sparkling
(and widely quoted) passages, most of the writing is worse than
the</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">Treatise</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">’s,
and the algebra just as dismal and irrelevant.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Paul Samuelson, professor of economics
at MIT after World War II and author of a best-selling economics
textbook, was one of Keynes’s most ardent American disciples. Here is
what he has to say about his beloved</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is a badly written book, poorly
organized. . . . It is arrogant, bad-tempered, polemical, and not overly
generous in its acknowledgements. It abounds in mare’s nests and
confusion. . . . \[It is\] an obscure book so that would-be
anti-Keynesians must assume their position largely on credit unless they
are willing to put in a great deal of work and run the risk of seduction
. . . \[by this\] work of
genius.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-5"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-5-back"><sup>5</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t32"
class="calibre4">In reading this, one recalls Keynes’s infatuation with
paradox.</span> Samuelson, the ardent disciple, is telling us that the
master’s book is good because it is bad.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t33"
class="calibre4">We do not, however, have to take Samuelson’s word
about</span> the bad writing, poor organization, and general confusion
of</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">. Following publication in
1936, many leading economists pointed to the same problems, although
some of them hesitated to criticize or quarrel with Keynes and thus
chose their words carefully. Frank H. Knight, a leading American
economist, complained that it was “inordinately difficult to tell what
the author means. . . . The direct contention of the work \[also\] seems
to me quite unsubstantiated.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-6"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-6-back"><sup>6</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Joseph Schumpeter, the most famous
economist of the mid-twentieth century after Keynes, noted Keynes’s
“technique of skirting problems by artificial definitions which, tied up
with highly specialized assumptions, produce paradoxical-looking
tautologies. . . .”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-7"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-7-back"><sup>7</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">British economist Hubert Henderson
privately stated that: “I have allowed myself to be inhibited for many
years . . . by a desire not to quarrel in public with Maynard . . . .
But . . . I regard Maynard’s books as a farrago of confused
sophistication.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-8"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-8-back"><sup>8</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">French economist Étienne Mantoux added
that the whole thing simply appeared to be “rationalization of a policy
. . . long known to be . . . dear to
him.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-9"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-9-back"><sup>9</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">itself, Keynes has a good word to say
about clarity, consistency, and
logic.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-10"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-10-back"><sup>10</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">He is quick to pounce on what he
considers the errors of others. But he then leads us down a rabbit hole
of convolution, needless and misleading jargon, mis-statement,
confusion, contradiction, unfactuality, and general illogic.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is not that Keynes is entirely
opaque. It is quite feasible to follow him down the hole and make out
what he seems to be saying. Having done so, it is not difficult to
extract and order his ideas in more intelligible form.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This has been our task up to this point.
We have sought to present Keynes, not in the murky way that he presents
himself, but as clearly as possible. It seemed important to do this,
first to reveal what Keynes himself really said, and then to muster
arguments</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">against it. In the
remainder of this chapter, however, we will very briefly explore the
rabbit hole itself, and in particular some of the rhetorical devices and
obfuscations that Keynes employed in order to defend the indefensible
and generally support his paradoxical policy recommendations.\
</span>

#### Device One: Obscurity

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t35"
class="calibre4">A typical sentence from</span></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We have full employment when output has
risen to a level at which the marginal return from a representative unit
of the factors of production has fallen to the minimum figure at which a
quantity of the factors sufficient to produce this output is
available.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-11"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-11-back"><sup>11</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This means, in essence, that we have not
reached full employment until all factors of production are fully
employed. We will recall that, per Keynes, only at this point do we have
to worry about inflation.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes took exception when other
economists wrote in this convoluted way. For example, in a 1931 letter
to the editor of</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The New Statesman and
Nation</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, he charged Lionel Robbins
with the same sin, even though Robbins was, on the whole, a very clear
writer:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t36"
class="calibre4">Professor Robbins wants “increased elasticity of
local</span> wage costs” . . . which means in plain English, I suppose,
a reduction of average wages.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-12"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-12-back"><sup>12</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Given this stab at Robbins, can we at
least assume that Keynes will avoid the term “elasticity” in</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">? No, not at all, he uses
(and misuses) it repeatedly.</span>

#### Device Two: Misuse of Technical Language

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the example above, Lionel Robbins was
at least using standard economist’s jargon. Keynes liked to make up his
own jargon, or worse, use standard jargon in a non standard way. This
led to a scolding by economist Frank H. Knight in the review of</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">that we have already cited: “Familiar
terms and modes of expression seem to be shunned on
principle.”</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-13"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-13-back"><sup>13</sup></a>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The only legitimate reason to use
technical language is to make a sentence clearer, if not to the average
reader, at least to the professional reader. Keynes habitually uses
technical language to confuse, and as we shall shortly see, this may
have been a deliberate strategy.\
</span>

#### Device Three: Shifting Definitions

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes tells us in</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">that economists have not clearly defined
the jargonish term “marginal efficiency of capital” (which roughly means
return on capital).</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-14"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-14-back"><sup>14</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">He then proceeds throughout the book to
use the term in many different ways, at least seven by Henry Hazlitt’s
count.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-15"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-15-back"><sup>15</sup></a>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Another slippery word in</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">is wages, which can mean an hourly rate
or total employee pay or something else. Keynes does not seem to notice
the difference, which leads him into serious logical errors.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t39"
class="calibre4">Once again, Keynes criticized the same lapse in others.
In a</span> book review early in his career, he took an author to task
for</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t40"
class="calibre4">us\[ing</span>\] the \[same\] expression some thirty
times in some apparently eight different
senses.</span><a href="#BackOther01.xhtml_ch20-16"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_ch20-16-back"><sup>16</sup></a>

#### Device Four: Misuse of Common Terms

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t42"
class="calibre4">In some cases, Keynes stretches the meaning of a
commonly used</span> word beyond recognition without explicitly
redefining it. For</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">example, he
tells us that for every commodity there is an implicit rate of interest,
a wheat rate of interest, a copper rate of interest, a steel plant rate
of interest, and so on. This confuses commodity options and futures
pricing with interest rates, a clear case of mixing apples and bananas.
We have already seen in chapter 16 that Keynes uses the word equilibrium
to describe what is actually disequilibrium.\
</span>

#### Device Five: Reversing Cause and Effect

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We will mostly treat this subject in
Part Five, when we return to the substance of Keynesian economics. But
it is part of the style as well. Keynes says that entrepreneurs
calculate how much revenue they will earn from x employees. But they do
not. They calculate how many employees they can afford from x
revenue.</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes says that prices
are low if production is low. In actuality, it is the reverse:
production is low if prices are low.</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes seems to like these reversals,
perhaps because they dress up the ordinary with a gloss of novelty, even
of profundity. But it is really no more than a parlor trick, and just
piles error on error.\
</span>

#### Device Six: False Determinism

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t45"
class="calibre4">Keynesian economist Alvin H. Hansen, whose
book</span></span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">A
Guide to Keynes</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">attempted to
de-mystify the master, tells us that “Keynes’s most notable contribution
was his consumption function.”</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">may be recalled from chapter 15, the
so-called marginal propensity to consume (consumption function) tells us
that people tend to save more as their income rises. Stated as such, it
is a commonplace, certainly nothing new. But Keynes calls it a
“fundamental psychological law,”</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">which it certainly is not. We can
neither predict with certainty that people will always save more as
their income rises, nor can we work out a forecastable schedule of
increased saving, as Keynes assumed.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">In the Keynesian model, the marginal
propensity to consume is also treated as an independent variable. (It is
supposed to determine other variables, not be determined by them.) This
is clearly false. As Benjamin Anderson, economist and early Keynes
critic, pointed out, “The so-called independent Keynesian variables (1.
The marginal propensity to consume, 2. The schedule of the marginal
efficiency of capital, and, 3. The rate of interest) are all influenced
by each other. They are interdependent, not independent. Keynes even
forgets himself and admits at one point that \#2 is influenced by
\#1.”</span>

#### Device Seven: Slipping Back and Forth between Mutually Inconsistent Categories

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We have already seen this in Keynes’s
use of words, for example when he uses the word “wages” to mean either a
wage rate or total wages. He is also prone to move back and forth
between physical commodities and services and money prices for
commodities and services, another case of mixing up apples and
bananas.</span><span class="calibre12 calibre3 calibre7 sgc-1"><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-44"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_nte-44-back"><sup>AAA</sup></a></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">\
</span>

#### Device Eight: Unsupported Assertion

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t48"
class="calibre4">In the entirety of</span></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, there are only two
references to statistical studies,</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">one of which Keynes partly dismisses as
improbable:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t49"
class="calibre4">Mr. Kuznet’s method must surely lead to too low
an</span> estimate.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Even when he discusses a subject that
especially lends itself to statistical analysis, such as a suggested
relationship between agricultural harvests and the business
cycle,</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">he simply takes a position
without bothering to search for relevant data.</span>

#### Device Nine: Misstatement

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">We saw an example of this in chapter 11,
which discusses Keynes’s mischaracterization of corporate sinking funds.
How could Keynes make such an elementary error? Probably because he had
said the same thing many times when speaking on his feet, and, being
busy, did not take sufficient time to check his written work.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Sometimes Keynes seems too busy even to
think. He says that if a lender lends money to a business owner, this
doubles the risk of a business owner using his own money, which doubled
risk is reflected in the interest rate.</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This makes no sense, as Henry Hazlitt
notes.</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">Risk is not doubled when a
lender enters the picture. The lender and the business owner share what
is still the same risk of failure.\
</span>

#### Device Ten: Macro or Aggregative Economics

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes is usually credited with
“inventing” macroeconomics, which looks at economy-wide flows rather
than the micro-economics of specific firms or industries. This is not
entirely accurate. Other economists adopted an economy-wide perspective,
although they often extrapolated from the firm or industry to the
economy as a whole, which Keynes wrongly criticized. Ironically, Keynes
attacked Say’s Law (see chapter 11) which is, itself, an example of
macroeconomics. It is certainly fair to say that Keynes developed his
own type of macroeconomics, which his followers developed into the
macroeconomics of today. It is also true that a macroeconomic viewpoint
makes it easier for a skilled casuist to mislead and confuse, and that
Keynes fully exploited this opening.\
</span>

#### Device Eleven: Misuse of Math

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t53"
class="calibre4">In chapter 15, we saw how Keynes wrote N = F (D), which
means</span> that employment, denoted N, is a function of demand.
Demand</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t54"
class="calibre4">however is defined as expected sales, not actual sales.
We noted</span> that expectations are not verifiable and thus do not
belong in an equation of this kind.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t55"
class="calibre4">Much of Keynes’s math is like this. As Henry Hazlitt
has</span> pointed out,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A mathematical statement, to be
scientifically useful, must, like a verbal statement, at least be</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">verifiable</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">,
even when it is not verified. If I say, for example (and am not merely
joking), that John’s love of Alice varies in an exact and determinable
relationship with Mary’s love of John, I ought to be able to prove that
this is so. I do not prove my statement—in fact, I do not make it a whit
more plausible or “scientific”—if I write, solemnly,</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t56"
class="calibre4">let X equal Mary’s love of John,</span></span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t57"
class="calibre4">and Y equal John’s love of Alice,</span></span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t58"
class="calibre4">then Y = f (X</span>)</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t59"
class="calibre4">—and go on triumphantly from there. Yet this is
the</span> kind of assertion constantly being made by mathematical
economists, and especially by Keynes.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t60"
class="calibre4">Given the</span></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t61"
class="calibre4">Alice in Wonderland</span></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">quality of</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">,</span><span class="calibre12 calibre3 calibre7 sgc-1"><a href="#Notes.xhtml_nte-45"
id="Chapter20.xhtml_nte-45-back"><sup>BBB</sup></a></span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">it should not surprise us that Keynes
interrupts his own misuse of math to tell us that he (apparently) agrees
with Hazlitt:</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">To say that Queen Victoria was a better
queen but not a happier woman than Queen Elizabeth \[is\] a proposition
not without meaning and not without interest, but unsuitable as material
for the differential calculus. Our precision will be a mock precision if
we try to use such partly vague and nonquantitative concepts as the
basis of a quantitative analysis.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t62"
class="calibre4">He also warns of</span></span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t63"
class="calibre4">symbolic pseudo-mathematical methods . . . of
eco</span>nomic analysis.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t64"
class="calibre4">After some of his own algebra he adds
that:</span></span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t65"
class="calibre4">I do not myself attach much value to
manipulations</span> of this kind.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">It is quite typical of Keynes now to
attack, now to disarm, now to shout, now to whisper, now to qualify his
mathematical claims, now to ignore, even blatantly ignore, the same
qualifications. On occasion, Keynes was even capable of a crude bluff.
Writing a private letter to Montagu Norman, Governor of the Bank of
England, he said that his theories (the same theories that would later
appear in</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The
General Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">) were a</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t66"
class="calibre4">mathematical certainty, \[not\] open to
dispute.</span></span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t67"
class="calibre4">Keynes certainly knew better. Some of his disciples did
not.</span> Economist Wilhelm Röpke noted in 1952 that</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">The \[Keynesian\] revolutionaries \[take
a stance of \] . . . vehement self-assertion and barely veiled contempt,
such as are habitual to the “enlightened” in dealing with those who
remain in the dark. They seem to regard themselves as all the more
superior in that they can point with obvious pride to the difficulty of
their literature and to the use of mathematics, which lifts the “new
economics” almost to the lofty heights of physics.</span>

![](Images/chpt_fig_005.png)\

<span class="dropcap">\
</span><span class="dropcap">\
</span><span class="dropcap">\
</span><span class="dropcap">\
</span><span class="dropcap">\
</span><span class="dropcap">\
</span><span class="dropcap">O</span>ne could go on, almost
indefinitely, citing Keynes’s obscurities, convolutions,
inconsistencies, factual or logical lapses, and so on, but it is time to
ask the obvious question: why did he write
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">this way? Keynes could be orderly,
organized, consistent, relevant, clear, complete, and factual, in
addition to being playful and witty, when he wanted to be. This is
apparent from the earlier books and many of the shorter pieces. There
are some snippets from</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">that also reflect these characteristics.
So why is most of</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">so different?</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">There are many possible answers.
Historian Paul Johnson has said, unrelated to Keynes, that “In financial
matters, the object of complexity is all too often to conceal the truth,
to deceive.”</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">The French economist
Étienne Mantoux, reviewing</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General Theory</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">shortly after publication, quoted an
earlier English economist, Samuel Bailey, from 1825: “An author’s
reputation for the profundity of his ideas often gains by a small
admixture of the unintelligible.”</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">This may be part of the explanation,
that Keynes intended to deceive or impress. But we must bear in mind
that Keynes was a salesman. He was trying to sell a particular type of
economic policy, and he was prepared to utilize any rhetorical device,
from crystal clarity and wit all the way to complete unintelligibility,
in order to make the sale.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Why would unintelligibility help to make
the sale? Not just because it can be used to impress. Equally important,
it can be used to intimidate. As we saw in the last chapter, Keynes (the
speaker) often made people feel, as his very intelligent friend Bob
Brand said, like “the bottom boy in the class.”</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter20.xhtml_t69"
class="calibre4">In this author’s opinion, Keynes developed obscurity as
one</span> of his speaking styles. He obscured, confused, and scrambled
the</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">mental “chessboard,” because
he felt confident that he could always keep the position of the “chess
pieces” in mind, and combine them as he saw fit for an attack in any
direction, whereas his opponents could not. This is a very impressive
skill indeed, especially when one is speaking extemporaneously. No
wonder that Sir Josiah Stamp, a very respected economist who often
partnered with Keynes on BBC broadcasts, said on the air that “I can
never answer you when you are \[verbally\] theorizing.”</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Whether this was a deliberate style on
Keynes’s part, or just a habit, we cannot know. But it was natural for
him to fall into the same scrambling, intimidating style when
writing</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The
General Theory.</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">The problem is
that it does not work as well in print as in conversation or debate.
When confined to print, it can be examined, examined minutely, and all
the myriad flaws, the errors of fact or reasoning, the rhetorical
tricks, the pseudo originality, may be revealed.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A few prominent economists, notably
Ludwig von Mises, Friedrich Hayek, Wilhelm Röpke, Jacques Rueff, and
Henry Hazlitt, among others, saw through it completely. Others perceived
that something was wrong, but hesitated to say so out of fear of
Keynes’s position and powers of retaliation. Regrettably, no major
economist published an immediate book-length refutation, so that the
influence of</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The
General Theory</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">spread and spread,
notwithstanding its all too apparent flaws.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Today many people—economists,
financiers, investors, business owners, and managers—say that Keynes is
their intellectual hero. Have they actually read</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">? Read more than the few
clear and witty passages so widely quoted? Read it cover to cover? Read
it from the perspective of mature years, not just while in
school?</span>

<span id="Part05.xhtml"></span>

# Part Five

# Conclusion

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml"></span>

## 21

## Upside-Down Economics: What Keynes Would Have You Believe

**<span class="dropcap">I</span><span class="small">N CHAPTER
1,</span>** we suggested that Keynesian economics defied common sense.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Keynes, as usual, seemed both to agree
and disagree. On page 349 of</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">The General
Theory</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, he extolled commonsense
and pilloried “orthodox” economists for lacking it. Perhaps he forgot
that on page 16 he had argued for an economics transcending simple
observation and logic, the elements of commonsense, and had pilloried
orthodox economists for being too simple.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Whatever Keynes may have thought about
common sense, he was not a commonsense economist. As we have seen, his
stock in trade was to take a commonsense proposition, for example that
savers are more likely to get rich than spenders, turn it on its head,
present it as a profound new insight, enjoy the gasps of astonishment,
and await the torrent of admiring applause. This is not, however, an
example of non-Euclidian geometry or the higher physics. It is an
example of the higher sophistry.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Thousands of years ago, the ancient
Roman philosopher Seneca warned about silver-tongued persuaders who will
talk us into believing almost anything. He observed that “Once you let
\[this\] sort of person . . . into your home, . . . you will have
someone regulating . . . the way you use your jaws as you eat, and in
fact going just as far as your patience and credulity
\[permit\].”</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">We did of course let
Keynes into our global economic policy home, and our credulity has been
stretched ever since. Consider, for example, a few samples from the
complete inventory of Keynesian or Keynesian-inspired paradoxes, partial
paradoxes, and challenges to economic commonsense:\
</span>

#### I. Keynesian Debt Paradoxes

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t76"
class="calibre4">A. If too much bad debt is the problem, for example
dur</span>ing the Crash of 2008, the solution is to add more
debt.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t77"
class="calibre4">B.</span> An economy depends on the players’
confidence. If confidence has been shaken by too much bad debt, restore
confidence by adding more.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t78"
class="calibre4">C. If the public has wasted money on unwise
borrowing</span> and spending, help them to borrow and spend
more.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">D. If government control of short-term
interest rates has driven down rates and encouraged too much bad debt,
nationalize all interest rates and drive them down too. (This is
essentially what the US Federal Reserve promised to do on March 18,
2009.)</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">E. After printing enough new money to
drive down all interest rates in order to encourage more borrowing, add
some new legal controls on borrowing so that no one borrows too
much.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t79"
class="calibre4">F.</span> If the public seems to be opposing the idea
of borrowing and spending more, their elected representatives in
government can do it for them.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">G. Better yet, let government develop
and announce a target for the amount of total borrowing we want each
year. (This is a proposal from Keynesian economists George Akerlof and
Robert Shiller.)</span> <span class="calibre6 calibre3">In general, we
should always start with the amount we want to borrow, not with what the
borrowed money will be used for.</span>

H. The more indebted an economy is, the more important it is to increase
borrowing. Alan Greenspan, former chairman of the US Federal Reserve,
argued this point in a 2006 interview.
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">President-elect Obama agreed in 2008:
“\[If the government does not intervene by printing money, borrowing,
and lending\], we . . . risk falling into a deflationary spiral that
could increase our massive debt even further.”</span>4

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">I. If interest rates are approaching
zero, they can still be driven down a lot lower. How? The US Federal
Reserve (or any other central bank) should hold the nominal rate at near
zero, but also create some “significant” consumer price inflation. This
way, real (inflation-adjusted) interest rates will be way below zero.
Just think: –4% rates? –6% rates? Nobody will be able to resist
borrowing at those kind of rates! (This is a proposal from distinguished
Keynesian-inspired economist Gregory Mankiw. He does not specify what
the “significant” inflation target should be. Others have suggested as
much as –6%.)</span>5

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t80"
class="calibre4">J.</span> Do not be confused by thinking of government
in traditional terms as a taker and borrower. Government is actually the
chief lender.</span>

#### II. Keynesian Spending-and-Saving Paradoxes

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A. Spending is the way to wealth. The
more we spend, the more we have. Stated as Keynes’s Law: If society
spends, goods will be produced. This refutes Say’s Law: If society works
and produces, it will have the means to buy what is produced.</span>

B. Each dollar of government expenditure magically multiplies itself as
it moves through the economy. Partly as a result, government deficit
spending pays for itself through lower unemployment payments and
increased revenues. Government debt burdens do not increase over time.
<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t82"
class="calibre4">(Reminder: Tell that to either the US or Japan,
but</span> especially the latter.)</span>

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t83" class="calibre4">C. We do not need to
save in order to invest.</span> (Reminder: This assumes that government
will print the money to be invested.)

D. New money printed by the government is a form of savings “just as
genuine” as traditional savings. But unlike traditional savings, it does
not cause slumps. On the contrary, it prevents and cures slumps. When
too much money is sloshing around the economy in the form of traditional
savings, government corrects the problem by printing new money (savings)
and injecting it into the economy. (For examples of this paradoxical
argument, see Paul Krugman, “Revenge of the \[Savings\] Glut,”
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">New York
Times</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, March 1, 2009, and Alan
Greens-pan, “The Fed Didn’t Cause the Housing Bubble,”</span>
<span class="calibre6 calibre3 calibre8 sgc-3">Wall Street
Journal</span><span class="calibre6 calibre3">, March 11, 2009.)</span>

E. Saving on a personal level may be prudent, even virtuous. But on the
national level, it is an antisocial act. So long as government prints
new money, we do not want more personal savings. Or if we do, not now.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t84"
class="calibre4">F.</span> Another solution to the problem of too much
savings is simply to work less. In this case, working less is the
responsible thing to do.\
(Reminder: Please do as Keynes says, not as he does, since he worked
hard and saved diligently.)</span>

G. If our puritan upbringing does not allow us to work less, “make work”
will serve just as well. Or we can hope for a natural disaster or even a
war to soak up savings.

#### III. Some Keynesian Style Paradoxes from President Obama

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A. Borrowing and spending is actually
saving and investing! The President said that his first budget, which
sharply racheted up borrowing and spending, “leads to broad economic
growth by moving from an era of borrow and spend to one where we save
and invest.” (This budget is called “A New Era of Responsibility.” It
represents a step away from the culture of “instant gratification,”
“excessive debt,” and “passing . . . \[a\] growing debt . . . on to our
children.” Stepped up spending and borrowing will “increase aggregate
demand.” This will “rebuild \[the\] economy, \[not\] on the same pile of
sand, \[but\] . . . on a rock.”)</span>6

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t87"
class="calibre4">B.</span> “Deficits . . . helped cause . . . this
crisis, \[but bigger deficits will help solve it\].”</span>7

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t88"
class="calibre4">C. Government spending programs not deemed
afford</span>able prior to a slump become affordable during a
slump.</span>

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t89" class="calibre4">D. In order to reduce
government spending on health care,</span> it is necessary to increase
that spending.

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t90" class="calibre4">E.</span> More
government spending is a reliable way to increase economic demand, but
it will not increase demand (and thereby raise prices) in health care.

#### IV. More Money Printing Paradoxes (In Addition to Those Already Covered under Debt)

A. Governments should not just drive down interest rates during a
crisis. As Keynes emphasized, they should keep interest rates down at
all times.\
(Reminder: Flooding the economy with new money eventually leads to
consumer price inflation. Consumer price inflation generally leads to
higher not lower interest rates. Alternatively, the new money may flow
into investment assets, in which case it produces asset inflation.
Either consumer price inflation or asset inflation typically leads to a
crash. Crashes reduce interest rates but not in the way intended.)

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t93" class="calibre4">B.</span> The eventual
target for interest rates should be zero. Stock dividends should also
eventually be zero.\
(Reminder: This means nothing is scarce and nobody has to wait for
anything.)

C. We should not worry about discouraging savers with low interest rates
(or high taxes on saving). As noted above, we do not want saving. Or if
we do want saving, low interest rates, not high rates, will produce more
of it. (This is an example of a Keynesian paradox within a paradox.)

D. Crashes are caused by credit problems related to inflation or debt
bubbles. Credit is controlled by the US Federal Reserve and other
central banks. Nevertheless, crashes are not caused by central banks.

E. Because it is difficult for national central banks to separate
themselves from politics, it would be better to have one world central
bank which will be free from politics.

#### V. Keynesian Economic Bailout Paradoxes

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A. Keynes was right: the private sector
is driven mostly by “animal spirits.” Crises are psychological in
origin. They just come when they come, with no particular rhyme or
reason.</span>

B. Keynesian economists George Akerlof and Robert Shiller help us better
define these nonrational “animal spirits.” They include, among other
elements, confidence, clashing views on fairness, and corruption.8
<span class="calibre6 calibre3">Viewed in this light, bailouts are a
multipurpose tool. They not only restore public confidence. They also
reassure the public that the economy is being run in a fair way and that
money is not corrupting public decisions.</span>

C. It is also reassuring to know that the government officials in charge
of bailouts and stimulus are free from fluctuating confidence, clashing
views on fairness, corruption, or animal spirits in general.

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t96" class="calibre4">D. If the largest
commercial and investment banks are</span> allowed to fail, there will
be no one left to lend.

E. Profits, even profits from highly speculative leveraged investments,
should accrue to private investors, but losses should be covered by
government and ultimately taxpayers. There are times when it is the
patriotic duty of small taxpayers to pay off the debts of rich
investors.

F. Because bailouts simply transfer money from one group in society to
another (e.g. taxpayers to rich investors), they are not economically
damaging. (This argument is offered by distinguished Keynesian Robert
Solow, retired professor of economics at MIT and Nobel Prize winner. As
he says, “\[These\] are not ethically satisfying transfers, but it is
not clear how they do long-term damage to the economy.”)9

<span class="calibre6 calibre3"><span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t97"
class="calibre4">G. Rewarding past speculative excesses will reduce
such</span> excesses in the future.</span>

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t98" class="calibre4">H. Rewarding failure
will increase the likelihood of future</span> success.

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t99" class="calibre4">I.</span> To make
bailouts work, it is best to rely on seasoned hands, the people who got
the companies in trouble in the first place.

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t100" class="calibre4">J.</span> If the
problem is that some companies are considered “too big to fail,” merge
failing companies into bigger ones, thereby making the survivors bigger
and bigger.

#### VI. Other Keynesian Government Economic Leadership Paradoxes

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">A. Wall Street is always a short-term
speculator.</span> Fortunately, as noted, government does not worry
about the next election but rather takes “long views.” This makes
government a more effective investor than the private sector. (Do not
worry that Keynes called politicians “utter
boobies.”<span class="calibre6 calibre3">That was just a private
joke.)</span>

B. Speaking of the advantage of “long views,” we must still remember
that any attempt to save is taking an over-long view. In this case, we
must remind ourselves that “In the long run we are all dead.”

C. When a company fails, it is supposed to die. When a government agency
(such as the US Federal Reserve) fails, we should give it ever more
power and ever more freedom from Congressional oversight.

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t102" class="calibre4">D. Although Wall Street
is a corrupt place, bringing gov</span>ernment in to run it will not
corrupt government.

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t103" class="calibre4">E.</span> Although Wall
Street is a mess, it is the best place from which to draw knowledgeable
and experienced people to run government economic and financial policy.

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">F. We do not have to worry about people
shuttling back and forth between jobs in Washington and in
Washington-run industries such as finance, medicine, and drugs.
Political-financial oligarchies only pose a threat to banana republics,
not developed economies.</span>

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t104" class="calibre4">G. The best thing about
government regulation is that</span> we never have to worry about who
will regulate the regulators.

<span id="Chapter21.xhtml_t105" class="calibre4">H. The banking industry
is already one of the most heavily</span> regulated industries, so more
regulation will fix it.

I. If banking goes back to being “boring,” as Keynesian economist Paul
Krugman recommends,<span class="calibre6 calibre3">all will be well.
Yes, times change. Old style “boring” banking at the mega bank level may
now be redundant—no longer needed. But not to worry. Government will
find a way to help it survive anyway.</span>

<span class="calibre6 calibre3">As noted above, this is a very partial
list of Keynesian paradoxes and partial
paradoxes.<a href="#Chapter21.xhtml_ast1"
id="Chapter21.xhtml_ast1-back"><sup>*</sup></a> What do all these
paradoxes mean? Is there any common theme to them? What is really wrong
here? These last questions bring us to the central paradox of
Keynesianism.</span>
